A method for constructing an installation accuracy control system for ultra-high-speed maglev supports considering known-point precision
Authors: Hongji Xu, Xuefeng Yang, Hang Luo, Liwei Zhang, Chenglong liu
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
To meet the extreme measurement accuracy requirements of the Polymorphic Coupled Rail Transit Dynamic Model Test Platform under complex design conditions—namely ultra-high speeds of up to 1,500 km/h and ambient pressures ranging from 0.005 to 1.0 standard atmospheres—this study addresses the lack of a systematic accuracy control theory for installation measurements of ultra-high-speed maglev vacuum-tube supports. As the fundamental positioning elements of the vacuum maglev tube system, support installations directly govern track smoothness and tube airtightness. However, a comprehensive accuracy control framework for this domain has yet to be established, substantially constraining reliable engineering implementation. Here, a “reverse” accuracy allocation strategy is proposed, taking support design parameters as the starting point and integrating the law of error propagation, Least Squares Collocation theory, and computer-based virtual observation simulations. This approach establishes a complete accuracy transfer chain, linking support design accuracy to measurement-control point accuracy, station-setting accuracy, and ultimately to the precision of the offline control network. On this basis, a three-dimensional free-station adjustment model for total station that incorporates the prior accuracy of known points is developed, effectively mitigating the influence of control-point errors on station-setting precision. Through dense grid-based simulation experiments, the spatial distribution characteristics of station-setting accuracy are systematically revealed, providing a theoretical foundation for optimal station placement in field operations. Case studies validate the feasibility and applicability of the proposed methodology, demonstrating that it delivers a rigorously derived accuracy control framework for measurement scheme design under varying tube parameters, instrument precisions, and control network configurations. The accuracy derivation system established in this work offers a complete technical pathway—from theoretical formulation to engineering application—for support installation measurements in China’s ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev transportation systems. It also provides an important reference for promoting the standardized and theory-driven application of precision measurement technologies in major engineering projects.

Introduction

With the continued advancement of China’s strategy to build a transportation powerhouse, the rail transit sector has achieved sustained and transformative progress, steadily moving toward higher speeds, greater intelligence and improved efficiency. By the end of 2024, the total length of the national railway network had exceeded 160,000 km, of which 45,000 km were high-speed railways (HSR), ranking among the world’s leading systems1. Driven by ongoing technological innovation, the pursuit of ever higher operating speeds in rail transportation has intensified. In this context, maglev rail transit has attracted widespread attention as a key development direction for “future transportation” 2,3.

The Polymorphic Coupled Rail Transit Dynamic Model Test Platform, a domestically pioneering and internationally leading innovative research facility, serves as a critical national platform for advancing research on ultra-high-speed vacuum tube transportation in China. It is distinguished by a long experimental track, comprehensive functionality, ultra-high operating speeds, multiple levitation modes, adjustable operating environments and variable model scales. The platform features an elevated test line with a total length of 1.62 km and a tube inner diameter of 3 m, within which the internal air pressure can be continuously regulated over a range of 0.005–1.0 standard atmospheres. With a designed maximum test speed of up to 1,500 km/h, the facility represents an advanced demonstration of “future transportation” technologies2.

Ultra-high-speed maglev systems impose exceptionally stringent requirements on track smoothness and vacuum tube sealing, with construction tolerances reaching the millimetre and even sub-millimetre level. As the fundamental components governing track alignment, the installation accuracy of vacuum tube supports directly determines the laying quality and operational stability of the track. Even minor deviations in support positioning can induce significant track geometric deformation under high-speed operating conditions, thereby compromising operational safety and the reliability of experimental data. Achieving high-precision installation measurement of supports is therefore essential. However, a mature technical framework for the installation measurement of maglev supports has yet to be established, and significant methodological gaps and practical challenges remain both domestically and internationally.

Owing to its flexibility in station selection and strong adaptability, free-station positioning with a TS has become a core technique in precision engineering surveying, including HSR applications, and vacuum maglev tube support installation is likewise based on this method. Existing studies have mainly focused on the optimisation of free-station geometric configurations and error propagation mechanisms. For example, Horemuz and Andersson examined the effects of the number and planar distribution of common points on station-point accuracy using a trial-and-error approach, and suggested that the optimal strategy is to place most common points near the TS, with only a small number positioned farther away4. Sun et al. simulated dual-backsight free-station experiments under different observation accuracies and confirmed that distance measurement errors exert a greater influence on positioning accuracy than angular measurement errors5. Regarding station-position optimisation, Horemuz and Jansson demonstrated that the optimal horizontal position of a TS should be located at the centroid of the control-point group, and that station position has no significant effect on the planar accuracy of measured object points6. Alizadeh-Khameneh et al. further investigated optimisation in the vertical direction and showed that instrument setup height has only a minor effect on the accuracy of the station-point elevation component7. In addition, the engineering applications of the free-station method have continued to expand. Shevchenko et al. applied it to building stability monitoring8, while Yang and Zou proposed an optimisation and accuracy analysis method for track straightness measurement9. However, most of these studies implicitly adopt the classical assumption that known-point coordinates are error-free, with insufficient attention paid to the propagation mechanism of known-point errors in three-dimensional (3D) space. In the context of HSR track geometric alignment measurement, Liu et al. overcame this limitation by accounting for the influence of prior known-point errors in the adjustment model for track geometry measurements using a track measurement trolley, from the perspective of 3D spatial coordinate transformation10.

Although previous studies have laid an important foundation, substantial research gaps remain for maglev tube engineering requiring ultra-high measurement precision. Existing literature has not yet provided a systematic analysis of the coupled mechanism among known-point precision, station-point spatial distribution and station-point accuracy, nor has a 3D free-station optimisation model for TS been established that fully incorporates known-point precision. Given the exceptionally stringent support installation accuracy requirements of vacuum maglev tubes designed for operating speeds of up to 1,500 km/h, even minute error sources cannot be neglected. Addressing these issues is therefore critical for establishing an accuracy control system for maglev support installation measurement.

On this basis, this study departs from the classical forward control paradigm in control surveying—namely, progression “from the whole to the part” and “from higher order to lower order”—and instead adopts a reverse-thinking strategy. Starting from the final accuracy requirements imposed by vacuum maglev tubes on support installation, the required accuracy indices for total-station setup and the corresponding measurement-region division scheme are derived in reverse. Specifically, this study first introduces the principle of Least Squares Collocation (LSC) to establish a 3D free-station adjustment model for TS that incorporates the prior precision of known points. Monte Carlo-based virtual simulation is then employed to systematically evaluate the spatial distribution characteristics of station-point accuracy under complex operating conditions. Finally, an integrated accuracy control system is developed, encompassing offline control network configuration, free-station accuracy control, and the determination of installation measurement and control point indices. The proposed method exhibits strong engineering adaptability. It enables dynamic calculation of measurement accuracy indices according to the design parameters of different vacuum maglev tube systems, thereby supporting a more scientific and rational control of accuracy throughout the entire process of maglev support installation.

Accuracy indices for support installation measurements

According to the design specifications of the Polymorphic Coupled Rail Transit Dynamic Model Test Platform, the installation accuracy design indices for vacuum tube supports are classified into two categories: absolute position parameters and support attitude parameters. The absolute position parameters comprise the longitudinal offset along the bridge axis (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{x}$$\end{document}), the transverse offset across the bridge (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{y}$$\end{document}), and the vertical offset (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{z}$$\end{document}). The support attitude parameters include the rotation about the bridge longitudinal axis (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{x}$$\end{document}), the rotation about the transverse bridge axis (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{y}$$\end{document}), and the rotation about the vertical bridge axis (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{z}$$\end{document}). The corresponding calculation formulations are given below.

A 3D coordinate system aligned with the tube axis is first established: a point on the vacuum tube axis is taken as the origin, the tube extension direction defines the x-axis, the direction perpendicular to the tube defines the y-axis, and the z-axis is normal to the x–y plane. All measurements in this study are conducted within this coordinate system. Owing to the specialised manufacturing process of the tube supports, their surfaces do not allow prisms to be mounted directly. Therefore, dedicated measurement fixtures are required in practical measurements. The fixture adopts a drawer-type design, as shown in Fig. 1, and is attached to the support in a prescribed manner. Four high-precision fixed prisms are installed at its corners. In Fig. 1a, P1, P2, P3 and P4 denote the support plate measurement points corresponding to the four prisms under the design state; in Fig. 1b, the light-blue upper plate represents the tube support, and the grey embedded component represents the measurement fixture. With sub-millimetre-level fabrication and assembly accuracy, the fixture ensures both a stable spatial relationship with the support and accurate relative positioning among the prisms. During support installation measurement, the TS directly observes the “spatial virtual plane” formed by these four prisms. Given the sub-millimetre accuracy of the fixture, this virtual plane can be regarded as strictly parallel to the upper surface of the support in space, and the attitude parameters it reflects can therefore directly characterise the actual attitude of the support upper surface.

Fig. 1Vacuum tube support and measurement fixture.

Vacuum tube support and measurement fixture.

As shown in Fig. 1a, the absolute position of a vacuum tube support is referenced to the centre point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{m}$$\end{document} on its surface. The 3D coordinates of this centre point are determined by averaging the coordinates of the four prisms mounted on the measurement fixture, that is:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}{x}_{{P}_{m}}=\frac{1}{4}({x}_{{P}_{1}}+{x}_{{P}_{2}}+{x}_{{P}_{3}}+{x}_{{P}_{4}})\\\:{y}_{{P}_{m}}=\frac{1}{4}({y}_{{P}_{1}}+{y}_{{P}_{2}}+{y}_{{P}_{3}}+{y}_{{P}_{4}})\\\:{z}_{{P}_{m}}=\frac{1}{4}({z}_{{P}_{1}}+{z}_{{P}_{2}}+{z}_{{P}_{3}}+{z}_{{P}_{4}})-v\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Where v denotes the relative height difference between the prism centre and the surface of the vacuum tube support, that is, the prism height.

The absolute position parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{z}$$\end{document} are calculated as follows:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}Dx={x}_{{P}_{m}meas}-{x}_{{P}_{m}des}\\\:Dy={y}_{{P}_{m}meas}-{y}_{{P}_{m}des}\\\:Dz={z}_{{P}_{m}meas}-{z}_{{P}_{m}des}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{{P}_{m}meas}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{y}_{{P}_{m}meas}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{z}_{{P}_{m}meas}$$\end{document} denotes the measured value, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{{P}_{m}des}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{y}_{{P}_{m}des}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{z}_{{P}_{m}des}$$\end{document} denotes the design value.

As illustrated in Fig. 2, m₁ and m₂ denote the transverse-axis centre points at the large- and small-mileage ends, respectively, while m₃ and m₄ represent the longitudinal-axis centre points on the left and right sides. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{L}_{x}$$\end{document} is the designed longitudinal distance between the measurement and control points of the fixture, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{L}_{y}$$\end{document} is the designed transverse distance between these points. The attitude of the vacuum tube support is controlled using three parameters, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{z}$$\end{document}, which are computed as follows:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}Rx={arctan}\frac{\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{3}-{m}_{4}}}{\sqrt{\varDelta\:{x}_{{m}_{3}-{m}_{4}}^{2}+\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{3}-{m}_{4}}^{2}}}\cdot\:\frac{\pi\:}{18{0}^{\circ\:}}\\\:Ry={arctan}\frac{\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}}{\sqrt{\varDelta\:{x}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}^{2}+\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}^{2}}}\cdot\:\frac{\pi\:}{18{0}^{\circ\:}}\\\:Rz={arcsin}\frac{\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}}{\sqrt{\varDelta\:{x}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}^{2}+\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}^{2}}}\cdot\:\frac{\pi\:}{18{0}^{\circ\:}}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Back-calculation of measurement and control point accuracy

Based on absolute position parameters

According to Eq. (2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{z}$$\end{document} represent the limiting errors of the respective coordinate components of the support centre point. In practice, the limiting error in measurements is commonly taken as twice the standard deviation. Accordingly, the designed installation measurement and control accuracy for the support centre point P should be defined as follows:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}{\sigma\:}_{Px}=\frac{1}{2}{D}_{x}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{Py}=\frac{1}{2}{D}_{y}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{Pz}=\frac{1}{2}{D}_{z}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{Px}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{Py}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{Pz}$$\end{document} denote the designed installation measurement and control accuracies of the respective coordinate components of the support centre point.

According to Eq. (1), the coordinates of the support centre point are obtained as the arithmetic mean of the coordinates of four independently observed prism points on the measurement fixture. By applying the law of error propagation11,12, the following relationship can be derived:5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{P}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4}}\sigma\:$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}}$$\end{document} denotes the standard deviation of each measurement and control point, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{P}$$\end{document} represents the standard deviation of the support centre point.

That is, the permissible limit for the measurement accuracy of each measurement and control point should be defined as follows:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}x}=2{\sigma\:}_{Px}={D}_{x}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}y}=2{\sigma\:}_{Py}={D}_{y}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}z}=2{\sigma\:}_{Pz}={D}_{z}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}z}$$\end{document} denote the installation measurement and control accuracies of the respective coordinate components of the measurement and control points on the fixture.

Based on the above expressions, the design values of the absolute position parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{z}$$\end{document} for the vacuum tube supports can be converted into the corresponding measurement accuracy indices of the measurement and control points on the fixture, thereby providing the basis for subsequent analyses.

Based on support attitude parameters

As shown in Fig. 3, panels (a), (b) and (c) respectively illustrate the attitude of the virtual plane formed by the centres of the four prisms on the measurement fixture when only the effects of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{z}$$\end{document} are considered. Based on the fixture characteristics described above, the spatial attitude of this virtual plane can be regarded as directly equivalent to the actual attitude of the upper plate surface of the tube support.

By substituting Eqs. (7), (8) and (9) into Eq. (3) and performing the necessary simplifications, the following result is obtained:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{3}-{m}_{4}}=Ly\cdot\:{sin}(Rx\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\\\:\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}=Lx\cdot\:{sin}(Ry\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\\\:\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}=Lx\cdot\:{sin}(Rz\cdot\:\frac{18{0}^{\circ\:}}{\pi\:})\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Using the above expression, the designed attitude parameters of the plate surface, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{z}$$\end{document}, can be converted into the corresponding incremental limiting values. Taking twice the standard deviation as the limiting criterion, the following relationship is obtained:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{3}-{m}_{4}}}=\frac{Ly}{2}\cdot\:{sin}(Rx\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\\\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}}=\frac{Lx}{2}\cdot\:{sin}(Ry\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\\\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}}=\frac{Lx}{2}\cdot\:{sin}(Rz\cdot\:\frac{18{0}^{\circ\:}}{\pi\:})\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Using the coordinate increment calculation formula, the following can be obtained:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Delta z_{m_3-m_4} &= z_{m_3}-z_{m_4} \\&= \frac{1}{2}\left(z_{P_1}+z_{P_2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(z_{P_3}+z_{P_4}\right) \\&= \frac{1}{2}\left(z_{P_1}-z_{P_4}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(z_{P_2}-z_{P_3}\right) \\&= \frac{1}{2}\left(\Delta z_{P_1-P_4}+\Delta z_{P_2-P_3}\right)\end{aligned}$$\end{document}13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Delta z_{m_1-m_2} &= z_{m_1} - z_{m_2} \\&= \frac{1}{2}(z_{P_1} + z_{P_4}) - \frac{1}{2}(z_{P_2} + z_{P_3}) \\&= \frac{1}{2}(z_{P_1} - z_{P_2}) + \frac{1}{2}(z_{P_4} - z_{P_3}) \\&= \frac{1}{2}(\Delta z_{P_1-P_2} + \Delta z_{P_4-P_3})\end{aligned}$$\end{document}14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Delta y_{m_1-m_2} &= y_{m_1} - y_{m_2} \\&= \frac{1}{2}(y_{P_1} + y_{P_4}) - \frac{1}{2}(y_{P_2} + y_{P_3}) \\&= \frac{1}{2}(y_{P_1} - y_{P_2}) + \frac{1}{2}(y_{P_4} - y_{P_3}) \\&= \frac{1}{2}(\Delta y_{P_1-P_2} + \Delta y_{P_4-P_3})\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

According to the law of error propagation11,12, the following relationship holds:15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{3}-{m}_{4}}}=\frac{{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{4}}}}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{2}-{P}_{3}}}}{\sqrt{2}}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}}=\frac{{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{2}}}}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{4}-{P}_{3}}}}{\sqrt{2}}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{y}_{{m}_{1}-{m}_{2}}}=\frac{{\sigma\:}_{{\varDelta\:y}_{P1-P2}}}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{{\sigma\:}_{{\varDelta\:y}_{P4-P3}}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

By substituting the above expressions into Eq. (11) and simplifying, the following result is obtained:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{4}}}={\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{2}-{P}_{3}}}=\frac{Ly}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\:{sin}(Rx\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\\\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{2}}}={\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{4}-{P}_{3}}}=\frac{Lx}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\:{sin}(Ry\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\\\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{y}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{2}}}={\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{y}_{{P}_{4}-{P}_{3}}}=\frac{Lx}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\:{sin}(Rz\cdot\:\frac{180^\circ\:}{\pi\:})\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Using the above expression, the limiting accuracy requirements for the designed plate attitude can be transformed into corresponding limits on the accuracy of the coordinate increments of the prism measurement and control points on the measurement fixture.

Back-calculation of free-station point accuracy

Back-calculation based on absolute position parameters

Based on the method described above, the design values of the absolute position parameters of the vacuum tube supports (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{y}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{z}$$\end{document}) can be transformed into the corresponding independent measurement accuracy indices (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{y}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{z}$$\end{document}) for each measurement and control point on the measurement fixture. During the installation measurement of ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev tube supports, a free-station TS configuration is adopted. Consequently, the installation measurement of an individual measurement and control point is essentially realised through 3D free-station positioning combined with polar-coordinate stake-out using a TS, as illustrated in Fig. 4. The stake-out point corresponds directly to the measurement and control point on the installation fixture.

Fig. 4Schematic illustration of stake-out using the polar-coordinate method.

Schematic illustration of stake-out using the polar-coordinate method.

According to the coordinate increment calculation formula, the coordinates of the stake-out point can be expressed as13:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}{x}_{p}={x}_{s}+D\cdot\:{cos}{\alpha\:}_{P}\\\:{y}_{p}={y}_{s}+D\cdot\:{sin}{\alpha\:}_{P}\\\:{z}_{p}={z}_{s}+S\cdot\:{cos}A\end{array}\right.$$\end{document} Where, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D=S\cdot{sin}A$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\alpha}_{P}=\omega\:+\alpha\:\pm\:36{0}^{\circ}$$\end{document}, then:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}{x}_{p}={x}_{s}+S\cdot\:{sin}A\cdot\:{cos}(\omega\:+\alpha\:)\\\:{y}_{p}={y}_{s}+S\cdot\:{sin}A\cdot\:{sin}(\omega\:+\alpha\:)\\\:{z}_{p}={z}_{s}+S\cdot\:{cos}A\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

By successively taking the total differentials of the above expression, the following result is obtained:19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}d{x}_{p} &=& \left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial {x}_{s}}\right)d{x}_{s}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \omega}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot d\omega+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S}\right)dS+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot dA+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot d\alpha \\&=& d{x}_{s}-\varDelta {y}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho}d\omega+\sin A\cdot \cos{\alpha}_{P}dS+\varDelta {z}_{SP}\cos{\alpha}_{P}\frac{1}{\rho}dA-\varDelta {y}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho}d\alpha\end{array}$$\end{document}20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}d{y}_{p} &=& \left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial {y}_{s}}\right)d{y}_{s}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \omega}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot d\omega+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S}\right)dS+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot dA+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot d\alpha \\&=& d{y}_{s}+\varDelta{x}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho}d\omega+\sin A\sin{\alpha}_{P}dS+\varDelta{z}_{SP}\sin{\alpha}_{P}\frac{1}{\rho}dA+\varDelta{x}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho}d\alpha\end{array}$$\end{document}21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}d{z}_{p} &=& \left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial {z}_{s}}\right)d{z}_{s}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S}\right)dS+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot dA \\&=& d{z}_{s}+\cos A\cdot dS-S\sin A\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\cdot dA\end{array}$$\end{document}

Rearranging the above expression and letting:22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:dx={\left[\begin{array}{cccc}d{x}_{s}&\:d{y}_{s}&\:d{z}_{s}&\:d\omega\:\end{array}\right]}^{T}$$\end{document}23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:ds=\left[\begin{array}{ccc}dS&\:dA&\:d\alpha\:\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{\alpha\:}=\left[\begin{array}{cccc}1&\:0&\:0&\:-\frac{\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}}{\rho\:}\\\:0&\:1&\:0&\:\frac{\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}}{\rho\:}\\\:0&\:0&\:1&\:0\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{s}=\left[\begin{array}{ccc}{sin}A\cdot\:{cos}{\alpha\:}_{P}&\:\varDelta\:{z}_{SP}{cos}{\alpha\:}_{P}\frac{1}{\rho\:}&\:-\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho\:}\\\:{sin}A{sin}{\alpha\:}_{P}&\:\varDelta\:{z}_{SP}{sin}{\alpha\:}_{P}\frac{1}{\rho\:}&\:\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho\:}\\\:{cos}A&\:-S{sin}A\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}&\:0\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}

Then:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\underset{3\times\:1}{\left[\begin{array}{c}d{x}_{p}\\\:d{y}_{p}\\\:d{z}_{p}\end{array}\right]}=\underset{3\times\:3}{{F}_{s}}\cdot\:\underset{3\times\:1}{\left[\begin{array}{c}d{S}_{1}\\\:dA\\\:d\alpha\:\end{array}\right]}+\underset{3\times\:4}{{F}_{\alpha\:}}\cdot\:\underset{4\times\:1}{\left[\begin{array}{c}d{x}_{S}\\\:d{y}_{S}\\\:d{z}_{S}\\\:d\omega\:\end{array}\right]}$$\end{document}

According to the law of error propagation12,13:27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{P}={F}_{S}\cdot\:D\cdot\:{F}_{S}^{T}+{F}_{\alpha\:}\cdot\:{D}_{S}\cdot\:{F}_{\alpha\:}^{T}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{P}$$\end{document} denotes the variance–covariance matrix of the stake-out point P, D represents the variance matrix of the observations, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{S}$$\end{document} denotes the variance–covariance matrix of the station point S. That is:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\underset{3\times\:3}{{D}_{P}=\left[\begin{array}{ccc}{{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}x}^{2}}_{P}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}y}^{2}}_{P}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}z}^{2}}_{P}\\\:{{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}x}^{2}}_{P}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}y}^{2}}_{P}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}z}^{2}}_{P}\\\:{{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{P}x}^{2}}_{P}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{P}y}^{2}}_{P}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{P}z}^{2}}_{P}\end{array}\right]}=\underset{3\times\:3}{{F}_{S}}\cdot\:\underset{3\times\:3}{\left[\begin{array}{ccc}{\sigma\:}_{S}^{2}&\:&\:\\\:&\:{\sigma\:}_{A}^{2}&\:\\\:&\:&\:{\sigma\:}_{\alpha\:}^{2}\end{array}\right]\cdot\:}{\underset{3\times\:3}{{F}_{S}}}^{T}+\underset{3\times\:4}{{F}_{\alpha\:}}\cdot\:\underset{4\times\:4}{{D}_{S}}\cdot\:\underset{4\times\:3}{{F}_{\alpha\:}^{T}}$$\end{document}

After calculation and simplification, the diagonal elements of the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{P}$$\end{document} are obtained as follows:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}}^{2}=({sin}A\cdot\:{cos}{\alpha\:}_{p}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{S}^{2}+(\varDelta\:{z}_{SP}\cdot\:{cos}{\alpha\:}_{P}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{A}^{2}+(\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{\alpha\:}^{2}+{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{S}}^{2}-2\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho\:}\cdot\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}+(\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}^{2}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}}^{2}=({sin}A\cdot\:{sin}{\alpha\:}_{p}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{S}^{2}+(\varDelta\:{z}_{SP}\cdot\:{sin}{\alpha\:}_{P}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{A}^{2}+(\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{\alpha\:}^{2}+{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{S}}^{2}+2\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}\frac{1}{\rho\:}\cdot\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}+(\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}^{2}\\\:{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{P}}^{2}=({cos}A{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{S}^{2}+(S\cdot\:{sin}A\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}{\sigma\:}_{A}^{2}+{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{S}}^{2}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

As indicated by the above expression, the accuracy of the measurement and control points is jointly determined by the observation accuracy and the accuracy of the station point.

Back-calculation based on support attitude parameters

As demonstrated by the foregoing analysis, the designed support attitude parameters can be converted into accuracy requirements for the coordinate increments \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{4}}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{2}}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\varDelta\:{y}_{{P}_{1}-{P}_{2}}}$$\end{document} of the measurement and control points. Consequently, precise control of the coordinate increment accuracy between measurement and control points enables effective control of the support installation attitude. Figure 5 presents a schematic illustration of staking out two measurement and control points using the polar-coordinate method with a free-station TS.

Fig. 5Schematic illustration of stake-out using the polar-coordinate method (two measurement and control points).

Schematic illustration of stake-out using the polar-coordinate method (two measurement and control points).

The coordinates of the stake-out points can be computed using Eq. (18) presented in the preceding section. Accordingly, the coordinate increments between the two stake-out points are given by:30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}\varDelta\:{x}_{{p}_{1-2}}={x}_{{p}_{2}}-{x}_{{p}_{1}}={S}_{2}\cdot\:{sin}{A}_{2}\cdot\:{cos}(\omega\:+{\alpha\:}_{2})-{S}_{1}\cdot\:{sin}{A}_{1}\cdot\:{cos}(\omega\:+{\alpha\:}_{1})\\\:\varDelta\:{y}_{{p}_{1-2}}={y}_{{p}_{2}}-{y}_{{p}_{1}}={S}_{2}\cdot\:{sin}{A}_{2}\cdot\:{sin}(\omega\:+{\alpha\:}_{2})-{S}_{1}\cdot\:{sin}{A}_{1}\cdot\:{sin}(\omega\:+{\alpha\:}_{1})\\\:\varDelta\:{z}_{{p}_{1-2}}={z}_{{p}_{2}}-{z}_{{p}_{1}}={S}_{2}\cdot\:{cos}{A}_{2}-{S}_{1}\cdot\:{cos}{A}_{1}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

By successively taking the total differentials of the above expression:31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:{x}_{{p}_{1-2}}=f({S}_{1},{A}_{1},{\alpha\:}_{1},{S}_{2},{A}_{2},{\alpha\:}_{2},\omega\:)$$\end{document}32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}d\varDelta{x}_{p_{1-2}}&=&\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S_{1}}\right)dS_{1}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A_{1}}\right)\frac{dA_{1}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha_{1}}\right)\frac{d\alpha_{1}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S_{2}}\right)dS_{2}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A_{2}}\right)\frac{dA_{2}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha_{2}}\right)\frac{d\alpha_{2}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \omega}\right)\frac{d\omega}{\rho}\\&=&-\sin A_{1}\cdot\cos(\omega+\alpha_{1})dS_{1}-S_{1}\cos(\omega+\alpha_{1})\cos A_{1}\frac{dA_{1}}{\rho}+S_{1}\sin A_{1}\sin(\omega+\alpha_{1})\frac{d\alpha_{1}}{\rho}\\&&+\sin A_{2}\cdot\cos(\omega+\alpha_{2})dS_{2}+S_{2}\cos(\omega+\alpha_{2})\cos A_{2}\frac{dA_{2}}{\rho}-S_{2}\sin A_{2}\sin(\omega+\alpha_{2})\frac{d\alpha_{2}}{\rho}\\&&+\left[S_{1}\sin A_{1}\sin(\omega+\alpha_{1})-S_{2}\sin A_{2}\sin(\omega+\alpha_{2})\right]\frac{d\omega}{\rho}\end{array}$$\end{document}33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:{y}_{{p}_{1-2}}=f({S}_{1},{A}_{1},{\alpha\:}_{1},{S}_{2},{A}_{2},{\alpha\:}_{2},\omega\:)$$\end{document}34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}d\varDelta{y}_{p_{1-2}}&=&\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S_{1}}\right)dS_{1}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A_{1}}\right)\frac{dA_{1}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha_{1}}\right)\frac{d\alpha_{1}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S_{2}}\right)dS_{2}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A_{2}}\right)\frac{dA_{2}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha_{2}}\right)\frac{d\alpha_{2}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial \omega}\right)\frac{d\omega}{\rho}\\&=&-\sin A_{1}\cdot\sin(\omega+\alpha_{1})dS_{1}-S_{1}\sin(\omega+\alpha_{1})\cdot\cos A_{1}\frac{dA_{1}}{\rho}-S_{1}\sin A_{1}\cdot\cos(\omega+\alpha_{1})\frac{d\alpha_{1}}{\rho}\\&&+\sin A_{2}\cdot\sin(\omega+\alpha_{2})dS_{2}+S_{2}\sin(\omega+\alpha_{2})\cdot\cos A_{2}\frac{dA_{2}}{\rho}+S_{2}\sin A_{2}\cos(\omega+\alpha_{2})\frac{d\alpha_{2}}{\rho}\\&&+\left[S_{2}\sin
