\usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:{z}_{{p}_{1-2}}=f({S}_{1},{A}_{1},{S}_{2},{A}_{2})$$\end{document}36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{array}{rcl}d\varDelta{z}_{p_{1-2}}&=&\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S_{1}}\right)dS_{1}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A_{1}}\right)\frac{dA_{1}}{\rho}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial S_{2}}\right)dS_{2}+\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial A_{2}}\right)\frac{dA_{2}}{\rho}\\&=&-\cos A_{1}dS_{1}+S_{1}\sin A_{1}\cdot\frac{dA_{1}}{\rho}+\cos A_{2}\cdot dS_{2}-S_{2}\sin A_{2}\cdot\frac{dA_{2}}{\rho}\end{array}$$\end{document}

By rearranging Eqs. (32), (34) and (36), and letting:37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:dP={\left[\begin{array}{ccccccc}d{S}_{1}&\:d{A}_{1}&\:d{\alpha\:}_{1}&\:d{S}_{2}&\:d{A}_{2}&\:d{\alpha\:}_{2}&\:d\omega\:\end{array}\right]}^{T}$$\end{document}38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:d\varDelta\:={\left[\begin{array}{ccc}d\varDelta\:{x}_{{P}_{1-2}}&\:d\varDelta\:{y}_{{P}_{1-2}}&\:d\varDelta\:{z}_{{P}_{1-2}}\end{array}\right]}^{T}$$\end{document}

It can be expressed as:39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:d\varDelta\:=K\cdot\:dP$$\end{document}

Where K denotes the coefficient matrix. Let the variance matrix of the observations be defined as:40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:D=diag\left(\begin{array}{ccccccc}{\sigma\:}_{{S}_{1}}^{2}&\:{\sigma\:}_{{A}_{1}}^{2}&\:{\sigma\:}_{{\alpha\:}_{1}}^{2}&\:{\sigma\:}_{{S}_{2}}^{2}&\:{\sigma\:}_{{A}_{2}}^{2}&\:{\sigma\:}_{{\alpha\:}_{2}}^{2}&\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}^{2}\end{array}\right)$$\end{document}

Considering the independence of the individual observations, and according to the law of error propagation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{\varDelta\:}=K\cdot\:D\cdot\:{K}^{T}$$\end{document}, the variance–covariance matrix of the coordinate increments is computed. Taking its diagonal elements yields:41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{\begin{array}{rcl}{\sigma}_{\varDelta x}^{2}&=&\left[\sin A_{1}\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{S_{1}}^{2}+\left[S_{1}\cdot\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\cos A_{1}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{A_{1}}^{2}+\left[S_{1}\cdot\sin A_{1}\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{\alpha_{1}}^{2}\\&&+\left[\sin A_{2}\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{S_{2}}^{2}+\left[S_{2}\cdot\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\cos A_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{A_{2}}^{2}+\left[S_{2}\cdot\sin A_{2}\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{\alpha_{2}}^{2}\\&&+\left\{\left[S_{1}\cdot\sin A_{1}\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)-S_{2}\cdot\sin A_{2}\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\right]\frac{1}{\rho}\right\}^{2}\cdot{\sigma}_{\omega}^{2}\\{\sigma}_{\varDelta y}^{2}&=&\left[\sin A_{1}\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{S_{1}}^{2}+\left[S_{1}\cdot\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\cos A_{1}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{A_{1}}^{2}+\left[S_{1}\cdot\sin A_{1}\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{\alpha_{1}}^{2}\\&&+\left[\sin A_{2}\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{S_{2}}^{2}+\left[S_{2}\cdot\sin\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\cos A_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{A_{2}}^{2}+\left[S_{2}\cdot\sin A_{2}\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right]^{2}{\sigma}_{\alpha_{2}}^{2}\\&&+\left\{\left[S_{2}\cdot\sin A_{2}\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{2}\right)-S_{1}\cdot\sin A_{1}\cos\left(\omega+\alpha_{1}\right)\right]\frac{1}{\rho}\right\}^{2}{\sigma}_{\omega}^{2}\\{\sigma}_{\varDelta z}^{2}&=&\left(\cos A_{1}\right)^{2}{\sigma}_{S_{1}}^{2}+\left(S_{1}\cdot\sin A_{1}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right)^{2}{\sigma}_{A_{1}}^{2}+\left(\cos A_{2}\right)^{2}{\sigma}_{S_{2}}^{2}+\left(S_{2}\cdot\sin A_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\rho}\right)^{2}{\sigma}_{A_{2}}^{2}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

According to the accuracy evaluation formula in Eq. (41), the accuracy of the x- and y-coordinate increments between the two stake-out points depends solely on the measurement accuracy and the accuracy of the station orientation angle, whereas the accuracy of the z-coordinate increment depends only on the measurement accuracy; none of these are affected by the coordinate accuracy of the station point. From a macroscopic perspective, as illustrated in Fig. 6, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}^{{\prime\:}}$$\end{document} denotes the orientation direction containing an error, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{P}_{1}}^{{\prime\:}}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{P}_{2}}^{{\prime\:}}$$\end{document} represent the two stake-out points influenced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}^{{\prime\:}}$$\end{document}. Under ideal conditions in which observation errors are neglected, both the lengths and horizontal directions of the two observation sides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:S{P}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:S{P}_{2}$$\end{document} are fixed quantities, and consequently the horizontal distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{{P}_{1}{P}_{2}}$$\end{document} between points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{P}_{2}$$\end{document} remains constant.

Fig. 6Schematic illustration of the effect of orientation angle errors in polar-coordinate stake-out.

Schematic illustration of the effect of orientation angle errors in polar-coordinate stake-out.

When an error exists in the orientation angle of the station point S, the relative geometric relationship between the two observation sides remains unchanged; instead, the configuration rotates as a whole about S, with the direction and magnitude of rotation corresponding to the orientation angle error. In this process, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{{P}_{1}{P}_{2}}$$\end{document} remains constant and, together with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:y$$\end{document}, forms a right triangle, leading to variations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:y$$\end{document}. Consequently, the accuracy of the coordinate increments between the two stake-out points depends not only on the observation accuracy but also on the accuracy of the station orientation angle. Although the orientation angle parameter enters the coordinate computation as a constant, it is in fact a random variable with known precision. Therefore, the orientation angle accuracy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}$$\end{document} of the station point must also be treated as an accuracy evaluation index.

In summary, by substituting the measurement and control point accuracy requirements derived from the support design parameters into Eqs. (29) and (41), the theoretical weakest coordinate accuracy and orientation angle accuracy required for the station point can be determined for instruments of different accuracy classes, while simultaneously considering the constraints imposed by both the absolute position parameters and attitude parameters of the support. This achieves the objective of the “reverse” accuracy allocation method proposed in this study: starting from the actual support installation accuracy requirements of vacuum maglev tubes and inversely deriving the required station-point accuracy with precision. The resulting framework enables the actual accuracy demand to be rigorously controlled and provides a solid theoretical basis for station-point accuracy design. It also ensures that the specified accuracy indices are scientifically and rationally defined, thereby avoiding both the risk of non-compliant construction caused by insufficient accuracy requirements and the reduction in construction efficiency or accuracy redundancy caused by overly stringent requirements.

A 3D free-station adjustment model for total station based on the principle of Least Squares Collocation

In HSR engineering, control points are commonly treated as error-free, non-random parameters in adjustment computations, an assumption that neglects the inevitable errors present in practice. For vacuum maglev tube systems with design operating speeds of up to 1,500 km/h, however, the installation accuracy requirements far exceed those of conventional engineering projects, and the influence of control point errors cannot be ignored. Moreover, the offline control points used in free-station positioning are themselves derived from adjustments of higher-order control networks and therefore possess prior accuracy information. They should thus be treated as random parameters with known prior precisions in the adjustment process. To properly account for the influence of control point accuracy, this study proposes a 3D free-station method for TS based on the principle of LSC thereby enhancing the reliability and precision of measurement and control in vacuum maglev tube support installation.

The principle of LSC is an adjustment approach that introduces random parameters (signals) with known prior expectations and variances into the functional model, while simultaneously accounting for both observation error variances and signal variances in accuracy assessment14. Its functional model is given by14:42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:L=AX+BY+\varDelta\:$$\end{document}

Where X denotes the non-random parameters (deterministic parameters), Y represents the random parameters (signals), L denotes the observations, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:$$\end{document} corresponds to the noise, that is, the observation errors.

The corresponding error equation is given by:43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:V=A\cdot\:dX+B\cdot\:dY-L$$\end{document}

Where V denotes the vector of observation corrections, dX is the vector of corrections to the parameters, and dY is the vector of corrections to the signals.

Computation of approximate station-point coordinates

In the 3D free-station method for TS based on the LSC principle, the original observations consist of the slope distances, horizontal directions and zenith distances between the station point and each control point. The station-point coordinates and orientation angle are treated as non-random parameters (parameters), while the control point coordinates are introduced as random parameters (signals). A functional model relating the observations, parameters and signals is established, and the parameter estimates are subsequently obtained through fitting based on the LSC principle. Consequently, prior to constructing the error variance model, approximate values of the parameters—namely, the approximate coordinates of the station point—must first be computed.

As shown in Fig. 7, the projection of point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\angle\:SCF{III}_{i}CF{III}_{j}$$\end{document} onto the x–y plane is given by15:44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\angle\:{S}^{{\prime\:}}CF{III}_{i}CF{{III}_{j}}^{{\prime\:}}={arccos}\frac{{D}_{i}^{2}+{D}_{ij}^{2}-{D}_{j}^{2}}{2{D}_{i}{D}_{ij}}$$\end{document} Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_{i}={S}_{i}\cdot\:{sin}{A}_{i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_{j}={S}_{j}\cdot\:{sin}{A}_{j}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${D}_{ij}=\sqrt{({X}_{j}-{X}_{i}{)}^{2}+({Y}_{j}-{Y}_{i}{)}^{2}}$$\end{document}.

Fig. 7Schematic illustration of approximate station-point coordinate determination.

Schematic illustration of approximate station-point coordinate determination.

The azimuth angle from point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$CF{III_{i}}$$\end{document} to point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:CF{III}_{j}$$\end{document} is calculated as:45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{T}_{i}}_{j}={arctan}\frac{{Y}_{j}-{Y}_{i}}{{X}_{j}-{X}_{i}}$$\end{document}

Accordingly, the azimuth angle from the station point S to point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:CF{III}_{i}$$\end{document} is given by:46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{T}_{Si}={T}_{ij}-\angle\:{S}^{{\prime\:}}CFII{I}_{i}CFII{{I}_{j}}^{{\prime\:}}\pm\:180^\circ\:$$\end{document}

Accordingly, the approximate 3D coordinates of the station point S are given by:47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{l}{X}_{S}^{0}={X}_{i}-{S}_{i}{sin}{A}_{i}{cos}{T}_{i}\\\:{Y}_{S}^{0}={Y}_{i}-{S}_{i}{sin}{A}_{i}{sin}{T}_{i}\\\:{Z}_{s}^{0}={Z}_{i}-{S}_{i}{cos}{A}_{i}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Construction of the adjustment functional model

Based on the 3D geometric relationship between the station point and the control points, the original observations—namely the distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{i}$$\end{document}, the horizontal direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{L}_{i}$$\end{document} and the zenith distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{A}_{i}$$\end{document}—their corresponding corrections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{v}_{Si}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{v}_{Li}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{v}_{Ai}$$\end{document}, and the coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({X}_{i},{Y}_{i},{Z}_{i})$$\end{document} of the control points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:CF{III}_{i}$$\end{document} as well as the station-point coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({X}_{S},{Y}_{S},{Z}_{S})$$\end{document} are related by the following expressions:48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{i}+{v}_{Si}=\sqrt{({X}_{i}-{X}_{S}{)}^{2}+({Y}_{i}-{Y}_{S}{)}^{2}+({Z}_{i}-{Z}_{S}{)}^{2}}$$\end{document}49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{tan}({L}_{i}+{v}_{Li}+\omega\:)=\frac{{Y}_{i}-{Y}_{S}}{{X}_{i}-{X}_{S}}$$\end{document}50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{cos}({A}_{i}+{v}_{Ai})=\frac{{Z}_{i}-{Z}_{S}}{\sqrt{({X}_{i}-{X}_{S}{)}^{2}+({Y}_{i}-{Y}_{S}{)}^{2}+({Z}_{i}-{Z}_{S}{)}^{2}}}$$\end{document}

The station-point coordinates are expressed as the sum of their approximate values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({X}_{S}^{0},{Y}_{S}^{0},\:{Z}_{S}^{0})$$\end{document} and the corresponding corrections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(d{X}_{S},d{Y}_{S},d{Z}_{S})$$\end{document}. Meanwhile, the control point coordinates are introduced as random parameters with known prior precision; accordingly, the control point coordinates are represented by the estimates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({X}_{i}^{0},{Y}_{i}^{0},{Z}_{i}^{0})$$\end{document} of their true values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({X}_{i},{Y}_{i},{Z}_{i})$$\end{document}, together with the corresponding corrections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(d{X}_{i},d{Y}_{i},d{Z}_{i})$$\end{document}.

Error equation for distance observations

By performing a Taylor series expansion of Eq. (48) and neglecting second- and higher-order terms, the error equation for the slope distance can be expressed as:51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{v}_{Si}=-\frac{{X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{i}^{0}}\cdot\:d{X}_{S}-\frac{{Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{i}^{0}}\cdot\:d{Y}_{S}-\frac{{Z}_{i}^{0}-{Z}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{i}^{0}}\cdot\:d{Z}_{S}+\frac{{X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{i}^{0}}\cdot\:d{X}_{i}+\frac{{Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{i}^{0}}\cdot\:d{Y}_{i}+\frac{{Z}_{i}^{0}-{Z}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{i}^{0}}\cdot\:d{Z}_{i}-({S}_{i}-{S}_{i}^{0})$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{i}^{0}=\sqrt{({X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}{)}^{2}+({Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}{)}^{2}+({Z}_{i}^{0}-{Z}_{S}^{0}{)}^{2}}$$\end{document}, that is, the approximate value of the slope distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{i}$$\end{document}.

Error equation for horizontal direction observations

In Eq. (49), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\omega\:$$\end{document} denotes the unknown orientation angle of the station point, expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\omega\:={\omega\:}^{0}+d\omega\:$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:d\omega\:$$\end{document} is the correction to the unknown orientation angle and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}^{0}$$\end{document} is its approximate value. The calculation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}^{0}$$\end{document} is given as follows:52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\omega\:}^{0}=\frac{{\sum\:}_{i=1}^{n}({T}_{i}^{0}-{L}_{i})}{n}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{T}_{i}^{0}$$\end{document} denotes the approximate azimuth of each backsight direction, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{T}_{i}^{0}={arctan}\frac{{Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{{X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}$$\end{document}, n denotes the number of backsight control points.

By performing a Taylor series expansion of Eq. (49) and neglecting second- and higher-order terms, the error equation for the horizontal direction observations can be derived as:53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{v}_{Li}=\rho\:\frac{{Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{({D}_{i}^{0}{)}^{2}}d{X}_{S}-\rho\:\frac{{X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}{({D}_{i}^{0}{)}^{2}}d{Y}_{S}-\rho\:\frac{{Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{({D}_{i}^{0}{)}^{2}}d{X}_{i}+\rho\:\frac{{X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}{({D}_{i}^{0}{)}^{2}}d{Y}_{i}-d\omega\:-({L}_{i}+{\omega\:}^{0}-{T}_{i}^{0})$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\rho\:={\frac{\left(180\times\:3600\right)}{\pi\:}}^{{\prime\:}{\prime\:}}\approx\:206264.80{6}^{{\prime\:\prime\:}}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}^{0}$$\end{document} denotes the approximate horizontal distance, expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}^{0}=\sqrt{({X}_{i}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}{)}^{2}+({Y}_{i}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}{)}^{2}}$$\end{document}.

Error equation for zenith distance observations

By performing a Taylor series expansion of Eq. (50) and neglecting second- and higher-order terms, the error equation for the zenith distance can be derived as:54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}v_{Ai} = &-\rho\frac{(X_i^0-X_S^0)(Z_i^0-Z_S^0)}{(S_i^0)^2D_i^0}dX_S-\rho\frac{(Y_i^0-Y_S^0)(Z_i^0-Z_S^0)}{(S_i^0)^2D_i^0}dY_S+\rho\frac{D_i^0}{(S_i^0)^2}dZ_S \\ &+\rho\frac{(X_i^0-X_S^0)(Z_i^0-Z_S^0)}{(S_i^0)^2D_i^0}dX_i+\rho\frac{(Y_i^0-Y_S^0)(Z_i^0-Z_S^0)}{(S_i^0)^2D_i^0}dY_i-\rho\frac{D_i^0}{(S_i^0)^2}dZ_i-(A_i-A_i^0)\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{A}_{i}^{0}$$\end{document} denotes the approximate zenith distance, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{A}_{i}}^{0}={arccos}\frac{{{Z}_{i}}^{0}-{{Z}_{S}}^{0}}{\sqrt{({{X}_{i}}^{0}-{{X}_{S}}^{0}{)}^{2}+({{Y}_{i}}^{0}-{{Y}_{S}}^{0}{)}^{2}+({{Z}_{i}}^{0}-{{Z}_{S}}^{0}{)}^{2}}}$$\end{document}.

Adjustment computation and accuracy assessment

By rearranging the error equations of the three types of observations according to Eq. (43), let:55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:dX={\left[\begin{array}{ccccccc}d{X}_{1}&\:d{Y}_{1}&\:d{Z}_{1}&\:\cdots\:&\:d{X}_{n}&\:d{Y}_{n}&\:d{Z}_{n}\end{array}\right]}^{T}$$\end{document}56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:dY={\left[\begin{array}{cccc}d{X}_{S}&\:d{Y}_{S}&\:d{Z}_{S}&\:d\omega\:\end{array}\right]}^{T}$$\end{document}

Where n denotes the number of control points.

By expressing the error equations in matrix form, the following representation is obtained:57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:V={A}_{3n\times\:3n}\underset{3n\times\:1}{\left[\begin{array}{c}d{X}_{1}\\\:d{Y}_{1}\\\:d{Z}_{1}\\\:\vdots \\\:d{X}_{n}\\\:d{Y}_{n}\\\:d{Z}_{n}\end{array}\right]}+{B}_{3n\times\:4}\underset{4\times\:1}{\left[\begin{array}{c}d{X}_{S}\\\:d{Y}_{S}\\\:d{Z}_{S}\\\:d\omega\:\end{array}\right]}-\underset{3n\times\:1}{\left[\begin{array}{c}{S}_{1}-{S}_{1}^{0}\\\:{L}_{1}+{\omega\:}^{0}-{T}_{1}^{0}\\\:{A}_{i}-{A}_{i}^{0}\\\:\vdots \\\:{S}_{n}-{S}_{n}^{0}\\\:{L}_{n}+{\omega\:}^{0}-{T}_{n}^{0}\\\:{A}_{n}-{A}_{n}^{0}\end{array}\right]}$$\end{document}

The signal coefficient matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{A}_{3n\times\:3n}$$\end{document} is given by:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left[\begin{array}{ccccccc}\frac{{X}_{1}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{1}^{0}}&\:\frac{{Y}_{1}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{1}^{0}}&\:\frac{{Z}_{1}^{0}-{Z}_{S}^{0}}{{S}_{1}^{0}}&\:\cdots\:&\:0&\:0&\:0\\\:-\rho\:\frac{{Y}_{1}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0}}{({D}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:\rho\:\frac{{X}_{1}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0}}{({D}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:0&\:\cdots\:&\:0&\:0&\:0\\\:\rho\:\frac{({X}_{1}^{0}-{X}_{S}^{0})({Z}_{1}^{0}-{Z}_{S}^{0})}{({S}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}{\cdot\:D}_{1}^{0}}&\:\rho\:\frac{({Y}_{1}^{0}-{Y}_{S}^{0})({Z}_{1}^{0}-{Z}_{S}^{0})}{({S}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}\cdot:{D}_{1}^{0}}&\:-\rho\:\frac{{D}_{1}^{0}}{({S}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:\cdots\:&\:0&\:0&\:0\\\:\vdots &\:\vdots &\:\vdots &\:\ddots\:&\: \vdots &\: \vdots &\: \vdots \\\:0&\:0&\:0&\:\cdots\:&\:\frac{{X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{n}^{0}}&\:\frac{{X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{n}^{0}}&\:\frac{{Z}_{n}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{n}^{0}}\\\:0&\:0&\:0&\:\cdots\:&\:-\rho\:\frac{{Y}_{n}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0}}{({D}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:\rho\:\frac{{X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{({D}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:0\\\:0&\:0&\:0&\:\cdots\:&\:\rho\:\frac{({X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0})({Z}_{n}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0})}{({S}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}\cdot\:{D}_{n}^{0}}&\:\rho\:\frac{({Y}_{n}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0})({Z}_{n}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0})}{({S}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}\cdot\:{D}_{n}^{0}}&\:-\rho\:\frac{{D}_{n}^{0}}{({S}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}}\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}

The parameter coefficient matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{B}_{3n\times\:4}$$\end{document} is given by:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left[\begin{array}{cccc}-\frac{{X}_{1}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{1}^{0}}&\:-\frac{{Y}_{1}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{1}^{0}}&\:-\frac{{Z}_{1}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{1}^{0}}&\:0\\\:\rho\:\frac{{Y}_{1}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0}}{({D}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:-\rho\:\frac{{X}_{1}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{({D}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:0&\:-1\\\:-\rho\:\frac{({X}_{1}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0})({Z}_{1}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0})}{({S}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}\cdot\:{D}_{1}^{0}}&\:-\rho\:\frac{({Y}_{1}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0})({Z}_{1}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0})}{({S}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}{\cdot\:D}_{1}^{0}}&\:\rho\:\frac{{D}_{1}^{0}}{({S}_{1}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:0\\\:\vdots&\:\vdots &\: \vdots &\:\vdots \\\:-\frac{{X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{n}^{0}}&\:-\frac{{Y}_{n}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{n}^{0}}&\:-\frac{{Z}_{n}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0}}{{S}_{n}^{0}}&\:0\\\:\rho\:\frac{{Y}_{n}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0}}{({D}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:-\rho\:\frac{{X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0}}{({D}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:0&\:-1\\\:-\rho\:\frac{({X}_{n}^{0}-{X}_{s}^{0})({Z}_{n}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0})}{({S}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}\cdot\:{D}_{n}^{0}}&\:-\rho\:\frac{({Y}_{n}^{0}-{Y}_{s}^{0})({Z}_{n}^{0}-{Z}_{s}^{0})}{({S}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}\cdot\:{D}_{n}^{0}}&\:\rho\:\frac{{D}_{n}^{0}}{({S}_{n}^{0}{)}^{2}}&\:0\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}

Based on the principle of LSC, the computation formula for the parameter corrections can be expressed as14,16:58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:dY=\left[{B}^{T}\right(D+A{D}_{G}{A}^{T}{)}^{-1}B{]}^{-1}{B}^{T}(D+A{D}_{G}{A}^{T}{)}^{-1}L$$\end{document}

Where D denotes the variance matrix of the observations, that is:59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:D=diag\left[\begin{array}{cccc}{D}_{1}&\:{D}_{2}&\:\cdots\:&\:{D}_{n}\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{i}=diag\left[\begin{array}{ccc}(a+b\cdot\:{S}_{i}{)}^{2}&\:(\frac{{\sigma\:}_{\alpha\:}}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}&\:(\frac{{\sigma\:}_{A}}{\rho\:}{)}^{2}\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{G}$$\end{document} denotes the prior variance matrix of the signals, that is, the variance matrix of all control points:61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{G}=diag\left[\begin{array}{cccc}{D}_{G1}&\:{D}_{G2}&\:\cdots\:&\:{D}_{Gn}\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{Gi}=diag\left[\begin{array}{ccc}{{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{i}}}^{2}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{i}}}^{2}&\:{{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{i}}}^{2}\end{array}\right]$$\end{document}

Accordingly, the adjusted parameter values—namely, the adjusted station-point coordinates and the orientation angle unknown—can be obtained using the following expression:63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:Y={Y}^{0}+dY$$\end{document}

Where Y denotes the parameter vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:Y={[\begin{array}{ccc}{X}_{S}&\:{Y}_{S}&\:\begin{array}{cc}{Z}_{S}&\:\omega\:\end{array}\end{array}\mathrm{]}}^{T}$$\end{document}; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Y}^{0}$$\end{document} is the vector of approximate parameter values, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{Y}^{0}=[\begin{array}{ccc}{X}_{S}^{0}&\:{Y}_{S}^{0}&\:\begin{array}{cc}{Z}_{S}^{0}&\:{\omega\:}^{0}\end{array}\end{array}{]}^{T}$$\end{document}; and dY is the vector of parameter corrections, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:dY=[\begin{array}{ccc}d{X}_{S}&\:d{Y}_{S}&\:\begin{array}{cc}d{Z}_{S}&\:d\omega\:\end{array}\end{array}{]}^{T}$$\end{document}.

According to the LSC principle10, the variance–covariance matrix of the parameter vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{Y}$$\end{document}, can be computed using the following expression:64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{Y}={\left[{B}^{T}(D+A{D}_{G}{A}^{T}{)}^{-1}B\right]}^{-1}$$\end{document}

By taking the square roots of the diagonal elements of the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{Y}$$\end{document}, the standard deviations of the individual coordinate components (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{S}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{S}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{S}}$$\end{document}) of the station point, as well as that of the orientation angle unknown (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{\omega\:}_{S}}$$\end{document}), are obtained. Accordingly, the positional standard deviation of the station point S, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{S}$$\end{document}, is given by:65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{S}=\sqrt{{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{S}}^{2}+{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{S}}^{2}+{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{S}}^{2}}$$\end{document}

Virtual simulation computation for 3D free-station positioning

Simulation computation is a computer-model-based approach for accuracy assessment. By designing the network geometry and control point coordinates, the theoretical values of the observations are back-calculated and superimposed with random errors that conform to the statistical characteristics of real measurement errors17, thereby generating simulated datasets that are equivalent to actual observations in both accuracy and distribution18. Although free-station measurements eliminate errors associated with instrument centring and height measurement, the variability of station locations makes the geometric configuration and resulting accuracy difficult to evaluate in advance. Consequently, only coarse accuracy requirements can typically be specified at the design stage, while practical implementation often relies on on-site trial calculations and, if necessary, repeated re-stationing, which increases workload and entails a strong dependence on operator experience.

Through numerical simulation of field observation processes, simulation computation enables a systematic evaluation of the accuracy of different free-station schemes during the engineering design phase. This allows the station region to be optimised and its expected accuracy to be estimated in advance, thereby effectively improving the efficiency and reliability of practical measurements.

Principles and implementation of simulation computation

Generation of pseudorandom numbers

The core of virtual simulation computation lies in generating pseudorandom numbers that follow a normal distribution, in order to simulate the random errors encountered in field observations in accordance with statistical principles. First, uniformly distributed random numbers are generated and validated, after which the Box–Muller transformation is applied to obtain a sequence of standard normally distributed random variables19. The Box–Muller algorithm is based on a two-dimensional normal distribution, in which the two marginal distributions, X and Y, are mutually independent one-dimensional random variables that both follow a normal distribution20. Accordingly, the probability density function (PDF) of this two-dimensional normal distribution can be expressed as the product of the PDFs of two one-dimensional normal distributions:66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:f(X,Y)=f\left(X\right)\cdot\:f\left(Y\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\:}}{e}^{\frac{-{X}^{2}}{2}}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\:}}{e}^{\frac{-{Y}^{2}}{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\:}{e}^{\frac{-({X}^{2}+{Y}^{2})}{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\:}{e}^{\frac{-{r}^{2}}{2}}$$\end{document}

The above expression indicates that this two-dimensional normal distribution can be described by a random vector r. In a polar coordinate system, r can be represented by two components—the angle θ and the magnitude r—that is:67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}X=r\cdot\:{cos}\theta\:\\\:Y=r\cdot\:{sin}\theta\:\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Assuming that X and Y both follow a standard normal distribution, integration of the above expression yields:68\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\int\:}_{-\infty\:}^{+\infty\:}{\int\:}_{-\infty\:}^{+\infty\:}\frac{1}{2\pi\:}{e}^{-\frac{{X}^{2}+{Y}^{2}}{2}}dXdY={\int\:}_{0}^{2\pi\:}{\int\:}_{0}^{+\infty\:}\frac{1}{2\pi\:}{e}^{-\frac{{r}^{2}}{2}}rdrd\theta\:=1$$\end{document}

Accordingly, the distribution functions of r and θ can be obtained as:69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}{P}_{r}(r<R)={\int\:}_{0}^{2\pi\:}d\theta\:{\int\:}_{0}^{R}\frac{1}{2\pi\:}{e}^{\frac{-{r}^{2}}{2}}rdr=1-{e}^{\frac{-{R}^{2}}{2}}\\\:{P}_{\theta\:}(\theta\:<\phi\:)={\int\:}_{0}^{\phi\:}d\theta\:{\int\:}_{0}^{\infty\:}\frac{1}{2\pi\:}{e}^{\frac{-{r}^{2}}{2}}rdr=\frac{\phi\:}{2\pi\:}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Where the angular component θ follows a uniform distribution over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left[\mathrm{0,2}\pi\:\right]$$\end{document}, let:70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{F}_{r}\left(r\right)=z=1-{e}^{\frac{-{r}^{2}}{2}}$$\end{document}

When the random variable z follows a uniform distribution over [0,1], the distribution function of r is given by Eq. (70). Because this function is strictly monotonically increasing, its inverse-function variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:(1-\mathrm{z})$$\end{document} also follows a uniform distribution on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left[\mathrm{0,1}\right]$$\end{document}. Accordingly, two independent random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{U}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{U}_{2}$$\end{document}, each following a uniform distribution U[0,1], can be selected, and let:71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\begin{array}{c}\theta\:=2\pi\:{U}_{1}\\\:1-z={U}_{2}\end{array}$$\end{document}

By substituting into Eq. (67), the uniformly distributed random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{U}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{U}_{2}$$\end{document} are transformed via the Box–Muller method into normally distributed random variables X and Y21:72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}X=\sqrt{-2{ln}{U}_{2}}{cos}(2\pi\:{U}_{1})\\\:Y=\sqrt{-2{ln}{U}_{2}}{sin}(2\pi\:{U}_{1})\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Generation of virtual simulation observation data

As described above, the original observation data consist of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{S}_{i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{L}_{i}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{A}_{i}$$\end{document}, and simulation of observations therefore requires modelling these three types of measurements. First, the 3D coordinates of the control points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:({x}_{i},{y}_{i},{z}_{i})$$\end{document} and their corresponding accuracy information (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{i}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{i}}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{z}_{i}}$$\end{document}) are obtained. A station region and station density are then defined, where the station region specifies the 3D spatial domain within which the instrument may be set up. Virtual station points are distributed within this region according to the prescribed density, yielding the theoretical coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left({x}_{S},{y}_{S},{z}_{S}\right)$$\end{document} of each virtual station point.

Based on the control point coordinates and the theoretical station-point coordinates, theoretical observation values are computed by coordinate back-calculation. Pseudorandom numbers following a normal distribution are then added to the theoretical observations according to the expression below, thereby generating virtual simulated field observation data.73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\left\{\begin{array}{c}{S}_{i}={S}_{i}^{0}+{\sigma\:}_{{s}_{i}}\cdot\:{x}_{i}\\\:{L}_{i}={L}_{i}^{0}+{\sigma\:}_{{L}_{i}}\cdot\:{x}_{j}\\\:{A}_{i}={A}_{i}^{0}+{\sigma\:}_{{A}_{i}}\cdot\:{x}_{k}\end{array}\right.$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{S}_{i}}$$\end{document} denotes the standard deviation of distance measurements, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{A}_{i}}$$\end{document} represents the standard deviation of zenith distance observations, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{L}_{i}}$$\end{document} denotes the standard deviation of horizontal direction observations; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{j}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{x}_{k}$$\end{document} are three independent normally distributed random variables.

In adjustment computations, random errors exceeding three times the standard deviation are generally regarded as gross errors22. Therefore, prior to error addition, values in the pseudorandom number sequence that exceed three times the standard deviation are removed to prevent the introduction of gross errors. Once the virtual simulated field observation files have been generated, station-point adjustment computations can be carried out using the adjustment model and accuracy evaluation formulas derived in the preceding section. The technical workflow of the 3D free-station simulation computation based on the LSC principle is illustrated in Fig. 8.

Fig. 8Technical workflow for Monte Carlo–based simulation of 3D free-station accuracy using LSC.

Technical workflow for Monte Carlo–based simulation of 3D free-station accuracy using LSC.

Accuracy evaluation of simulation computation and the adjustment model

Simulation experiment design

To validate the effectiveness of the simulation experiments and to analyse the accuracy characteristics of the 3D free-station adjustment model based on the LSC principle, the following experimental scheme was designed. The experimental network geometry was established in accordance with the design standards of track control networks (CPⅢ) used in HSR engineering measurements. A point-pair network configuration was adopted, with a spacing of 60 m between adjacent point pairs and a transverse separation of 20 m between the two control points within each pair23,24. A total of four pairs of control points were deployed within the experimental area. The spatial distribution of the control points is illustrated in Fig. 9, and their coordinate information is listed in Table 1.

Fig. 9Schematic distribution of the designed control-point coordinates for the simulation experiment.

Schematic distribution of the designed control-point coordinates for the simulation experiment.

and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{z}$$\end{document} denotes the design value of the standard deviation of the z-coordinate component of the control point.

Control point coordinates and accuracies for the simulation experiment.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{x}$$\end{document}denotes the design value of the standard deviation of the x-coordinate component of the control point; \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{y}$$\end{document} denotes the design value of the standard deviation of the y-coordinate component of the control point; and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{z}$$\end{document} denotes the design value of the standard deviation of the z-coordinate component of the control point.

The station region was defined to cover the coordinate range of the control points and was extended outward by 30 m in both the x and y directions and by 60 m in the z direction. Within this region, station points were uniformly distributed at intervals of 5 m, and observations to all eight control points were conducted from each station. The TS measurement precision adopted in the simulation experiments was specified as a distance measurement accuracy of 0.6 mm + 1 ppm and an angular measurement accuracy of 0.5″.

Analysis of the correctness of pseudorandom number incorporation in simulation computation

The core of simulation computation lies in the modelling of random errors, the accuracy of which directly determines whether the simulation experiments can faithfully represent actual measurement conditions. Prior to the accuracy analysis of the adjustment model, statistical tests were first conducted on the simulated errors of the three types of observations. In accordance with the experimental design, simulation computations were performed using the technical workflow described above, and a total of 166,536 simulated errors were extracted for each of the distance, horizontal angle and zenith distance observations. Error-frequency histograms were then constructed and fitted with their corresponding probability density curves, and the mathematical expectation and variance of each error dataset were computed. The results are presented in Fig. 10.

Fig. 10Distribution of observation errors.

Distribution of observation errors.

In classical error theory, random errors are regarded as random variables following a normal distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathrm{N}(0,{{\upsigma\:}}^{2})$$\end{document},25 that is, with a mathematical expectation of zero and a variance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}^{2}$$\end{document}. According to the simulation experiment design, the TS distance measurement accuracy is specified as 0.6 mm + 1 ppm, and the angular measurement accuracy as 0.5″. In the simulations, these nominal accuracies are adopted as the basis for error modelling. Consequently, the errors of the horizontal direction and zenith distance observations are expected to follow a normal distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathrm{N}(0,{0.5}^{2})$$\end{document}.Because the distance measurement accuracy includes a proportional error component, the variance of the distance error distribution is, in principle, non-constant. For quantitative analysis, the mean value of all distance observations (95.4277 m) is used for approximation. Accordingly, the distance observation errors are expected to approximately follow a normal distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\mathrm{N}(0,{0.6954}^{2})$$\end{document}.

The statistical results of the simulated errors indicate that the mean value of the simulated distance observation errors is 0.00101 mm, with a standard deviation of 0.68835 mm, differing from the theoretical values by only 0.00101 mm and 0.00705 mm, respectively. For the simulated horizontal direction observations, the mean error is −0.00131″ and the standard deviation is 0.49369″, with deviations from the theoretical values of merely 0.00131″ and 0.00631″, respectively. Similarly, the simulated zenith distance observation errors exhibit a mean of −0.00239″ and a standard deviation of 0.49317″, differing from the theoretical values by only 0.00239″ and 0.00683″, respectively. For all three types of observations, the discrepancies between the simulated and theoretical values of both the mean and the standard deviation are on the order of 10− 3, demonstrating that the error simulation performs well and is sufficient to meet the accuracy requirements of the subsequent experimental analyses.

Accuracy analysis of the adjustment model

To compare the differences in absolute coordinate accuracy of station points between the 3D free-station adjustment models for TS based on the Least Squares Collocation (LSC) principle and the conventional Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) principle, both models were applied to the same set of virtual simulated field observation data. To eliminate the influence of approximate coordinates on the adjustment results, identical methods for computing the initial approximate coordinates were adopted for both models. Accuracy evaluation was primarily based on the true coordinate error \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:$$\end{document} of each adjusted station point. The mathematical expectation of the absolute true coordinate errors across all station points, denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(\varDelta\:\right)$$\end{document}, was further used as a quantitative indicator of model accuracy. The corresponding calculation formula is given below.74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:={\stackrel{\sim}{L}}_{i}-{L}_{i}$$\end{document}

Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\varDelta\:$$\end{document} denotes the true error, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\stackrel{\sim}{L}}_{i}$$\end{document} represents the adjusted coordinate value of the station point, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{L}_{i}$$\end{document} denotes the true coordinate value of the station point.75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(\varDelta\:\right)=\frac{1}{n}{\sum\:}_{i=1}^{n}\left|\varDelta\:\right|$$\end{document}

Where n denotes the number of station points.

In practical engineering applications, the true coordinate errors of control points are difficult to determine accurately. To examine the performance of the adjustment model under extreme error conditions, several limiting error modes were imposed on the control points, including control network distortion, in which the control-point errors are distributed with alternating positive and negative signs; control network expansion, in which the errors cause the network geometry to expand uniformly outward; control network contraction, in which the errors cause the network geometry to converge uniformly inward; control network translation, in which the errors induce a systematic shift of the network geometry; and an error-free control network, in which no control-point errors are applied. In all modes, three times the standard deviation of the control points was adopted as the limiting error threshold. The control-point coordinates under each mode are listed in Table 2, and schematic diagrams of the point distributions are shown in Fig. 11.
