\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{(+3{\upsigma\:}}_{z})$$\end{document}

480.5466

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{(+3{\upsigma\:}}_{z})$$\end{document}

480.5387

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{(+3{\upsigma\:}}_{z})$$\end{document}

Fig. 11Schematic distribution of control points under different error modes in the simulation experiment.

Schematic distribution of control points under different error modes in the simulation experiment.

To eliminate the potential influence of elevation differences on the results, a grouped analysis was conducted using three elevation levels within the experimental design region: the 540 m elevation plane (the upper boundary of the station region), the 480 m elevation plane (the elevation of the control points), and the 420 m elevation plane (the lower boundary of the station region). The statistical results for each group are presented in Fig. 12.

Fig. 12Expected absolute true coordinate errors under different adjustment models.

Expected absolute true coordinate errors under different adjustment models.

The experimental results indicate that, when control network distortion is induced by true coordinate errors of the control points, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(\varDelta\:\right)$$\end{document} values obtained from the LSC adjustment model are smaller than those from the OLS model across all three elevation planes. The most pronounced improvements are observed on the 480 m elevation plane, where all three coordinate components are located. Specifically, the x-component error is reduced from 3.72 mm under the OLS model to 0.63 mm with the LSC model, corresponding to a reduction of 83.06%; the y-component error decreases from 3.64 mm to 1.41 mm, a reduction of 61.26%; and the z-component error is reduced from 2.30 mm to 0.06 mm, achieving a reduction of 97.39%.

When control network expansion occurs, the reduction in the x-component error is relatively consistent across the three elevation planes, with the most pronounced improvement observed at the 540 m elevation plane, where the error decreases from 4.54 mm to 0.62 mm, corresponding to a reduction of 86.34%. At the 480 m elevation plane, the x-component error is reduced from 4.87 mm to 0.70 mm, representing an 85.62% decrease. For the y-component, the error reductions at the 420 m and 540 m elevation planes are comparable, decreasing from 2.99 mm to 2.98 mm to 1.18 mm, corresponding to reductions of 60.53% and 60.40%, respectively. At the 480 m elevation plane, the y-component error decreases from 3.34 mm to 1.43 mm, yielding a reduction of 57.18%. In contrast, for the z-component, the LSC adjustment result at the 420 m elevation plane (6.03 mm) is higher than that obtained with the OLS model (3.27 mm), whereas at the 540 m elevation plane, the LSC result (5.96 mm) is lower than the OLS result (9.24 mm). At the elevation plane corresponding to the control points (480 m), the results from the two models are essentially comparable.

Under control network contraction, the trends in error variation are similar to those observed for network expansion. When the control network undergoes overall translation, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(\varDelta\:\right)$$\end{document} values obtained from the two adjustment methods are essentially identical across all three elevation planes. Under the ideal condition in which the control points are free of true coordinate errors, both methods yield favourable adjustment results, with the vast majority of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:E\left(\varDelta\:\right)$$\end{document} values being less than 1 mm. The only exception occurs for the y-component at the 480 m elevation plane, where the LSC adjustment result reaches 1.18 mm.

In summary, under various extreme error conditions affecting the control points, the LSC-based model outperforms the OLS model in terms of accuracy in most scenarios, with substantial improvements observed. Therefore, for engineering applications such as the installation of ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev tube supports, where millimetre- or even sub-millimetre-level accuracy is required, control point errors cannot be neglected, and the conventional practice of ignoring them in traditional measurements is no longer appropriate.

Spatial distribution of 3D free-station coordinate accuracy based on the LSC principle

To investigate the accuracy distribution characteristics of total-station free-station positioning based on the LSC model, and thereby to guide subsequent optimisation of offline control network geometry and the selection of station regions, this study integrates the LSC-based accuracy evaluation method and simulation theory described above with the experimental design. Virtual observations to eight control points were performed at each of 20,817 predefined station points within the experimental region, and free-station adjustment and accuracy assessment were carried out using the LSC model. Drawing on concepts from differential and integral analysis, the entire station region was discretised into 20,817 independent station units, each represented by a single station point, to enable simulation-based evaluation of the overall spatial accuracy distribution. Because the station region has a 3D cubic structure, the internal accuracy distribution is difficult to visualise directly. For clarity of analysis, three representative cross-sections were therefore selected along the x–y, x–z and y–z projection directions: the upper surface (Section A), the middle layer (Section B) and the lower surface (Section C). Schematic illustrations of these sections are shown in Fig. 13.

Fig. 13Schematic illustration of the selected cross-sections.

Schematic illustration of the selected cross-sections.

For each cross-section, heat maps were generated for the standard deviation of the x-coordinate component (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{x}$$\end{document}), the standard deviation of the y-coordinate component (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{y}$$\end{document}), the standard deviation of the z-coordinate component (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{z}$$\end{document}), the positional standard deviation of the station point (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{P}$$\end{document}), and the standard deviation of the orientation angle (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{{\upomega\:}}$$\end{document}), enabling a systematic analysis of the spatial distribution characteristics of these accuracy metrics. The corresponding results are presented in Figs. 14, 15 and 16.

Fig. 14Heat maps of station-point accuracy distribution for the x–y cross-sections.

Heat maps of station-point accuracy distribution for the x–y cross-sections.

Based on the results from the three cross-sections projected onto the x–y plane, the distribution of station-point accuracy is closely related to the planar distribution of the control points. With the exception of the standard deviation of the orientation angle (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{{\upomega\:}}$$\end{document}), the spatial distributions of the accuracy metrics across Sections A, B and C are broadly consistent. Specifically, the distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{x}$$\end{document} is symmetric about the centreline in the y-direction of the control-point projection onto the x–y plane and increases progressively toward both sides. In contrast, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{y}$$\end{document} is symmetric about the centreline in the x-direction of the same projection and similarly expands outward. The distributions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{z}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{P}$$\end{document} exhibit identical patterns, both forming concentric contours that increase radially outward from the geometric centre of the control-point projection. The distribution pattern of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{{\upomega\:}}$$\end{document} differs from the others: in Sections A and C it diverges outward in an approximately elliptical form, whereas in Section B it expands laterally along the x-direction centreline of the control-point projection.

Fig. 15Heat maps of station-point accuracy distribution for the x–z cross-sections.

Heat maps of station-point accuracy distribution for the x–z cross-sections.

To further examine the influence of elevation on station-point accuracy, slices projected onto the x–z and y–z planes were analysed. The results for the x–z sections indicate that station elevation has no significant effect on accuracy in this projection. Although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{x}$$\end{document} exhibits an approximately elliptical distribution centred on the geometric centre of the control-point projection, the overall variation in magnitude is small. By contrast, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{y}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{z}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{P}$$\end{document} display vertically banded distributions, indicating that variations in z at a fixed x coordinate exert only a weak influence on these accuracy metrics. The distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{{\upomega\:}}$$\end{document} in Sections A and C is elliptical, with the major axis oriented along the z direction and the minor axis along the x direction, and with the major axis longer than the minor axis. Overall, the pattern still diverges primarily along the x direction, further demonstrating the limited impact of elevation changes on accuracy. In Section B, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{{\upomega\:}}$$\end{document} exhibits a vertically banded distribution, suggesting that variations in station elevation within this section have a negligible effect on orientation angle accuracy.

Fig. 16Heat maps of station-point accuracy distribution for the y–z cross-sections.

Heat maps of station-point accuracy distribution for the y–z cross-sections.

Similarly, in the y–z projection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{x}$$\end{document} exhibits a vertically banded distribution, indicating that elevation variations in this direction have only a weak influence on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{x}$$\end{document}. Although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{y}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{z}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{P}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{{\upomega\:}}$$\end{document} display banded patterns of different forms, the colour variations within each cross-section are small, suggesting limited accuracy fluctuations within individual sections. This further indicates that elevation has no significant effect on these accuracy metrics.

Synthesising the heat map analyses across the three projection directions and multiple cross-sections indicates that the spatial distribution of station-point accuracy is governed primarily by the planar distribution of control points in the x–y projection, while exhibiting little dependence on the elevation of the station points. Consequently, as discussed earlier, the station region can be selected on the bridge piers, which ensures favourable fields of view for vacuum tube support installation measurements while having a negligible impact on measurement accuracy.

In summary, the Monte Carlo–based simulation computation of 3D free-station accuracy using LSC enables a critical step in the proposed “reverse” accuracy allocation chain: under prescribed station-point accuracy requirements, the optimal configuration of the control network can be inversely derived. By conducting virtual simulation experiments that jointly consider multiple factors, including control-point distribution, prior accuracy level and instrument observation precision, this method can not only quantitatively evaluate and identify the optimal control network layout, but also precisely delineate the theoretically admissible station regions. This closed-loop validation mechanism overcomes the limitations of conventional one-way accuracy propagation and provides a refined quantitative basis for decision-making in control network configuration. It ensures the scientific rationality of control network design while substantially improving the flexibility and reliability of station-point selection.

Case study

Through the rigorous derivations and calculations presented above, a complete accuracy back-propagation framework is established, linking the absolute position design parameters (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{D}_{z}$$\end{document}) and attitude design parameters (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{y}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{R}_{z}$$\end{document}) of vacuum maglev tube supports to the permissible measurement accuracy limits of the prism measurement and control points on the installation fixture. This framework is further extended to determine the limiting requirements for station-point coordinate accuracy and orientation angle accuracy. By incorporating virtual simulation computation, the distribution and accuracy design of the offline control points can be systematically assessed and controlled. As a result, a comprehensive accuracy limit derivation system for ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev tube support installation measurements is achieved, explicitly accounting for the precision of known points and providing a rigorous theoretical foundation for the design of measurement schemes.

A case study is presented below based on the design parameters of a vacuum maglev tube support to verify the effectiveness of the proposed method. The relevant design parameters are as follows:

Station-point accuracy computation

Based on the permissible measurement and control limits of attitude parameters

As noted earlier, to ensure an adequate field of view for installation measurements, the station point is selected on the top surface of a bridge pier where no support has yet been installed. In the derivation of the accuracy indices, calculations are performed with respect to the centre point of the pier, as illustrated in Fig. 18.

Fig. 18Schematic illustration of tube support installation measurement.

Schematic illustration of tube support installation measurement.

The TS setup height was set to 1.5 m. The calculation results are summarised in Table 3.

Based on the permissible measurement and control limits of absolute position parameters

According to Eq. (29), the accuracy of the measurement and control points is jointly determined by the observation accuracy and the station-point accuracy, and also involves the covariance terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document}. Extensive virtual simulation adjustment computations indicate that the order of magnitude of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:1{0}^{-3}$$\end{document}, while that of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-1{0}^{-3}$$\end{document}. The coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-2\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}$$\end{document}; considering the spatial configuration of the station point S and the measurement and control point P, calculations show that the order of magnitude of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-2\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-1{0}^{-3}$$\end{document}. Consequently, the contribution of the covariance term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-2\varDelta\:{y}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}\cdot\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} to the variance of the x-coordinate of the measurement and control point, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}}^{2}$$\end{document}, is on the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-1{0}^{-6}$$\end{document}.

Similarly, the coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:2\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}$$\end{document}; based on the spatial relationship between the station point S and the observation point P, the order of magnitude of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:2\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}$$\end{document} is approximately \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:1{0}^{-1}$$\end{document}. Accordingly, the contribution of the covariance term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:2\varDelta\:{x}_{SP}\cdot\:\frac{1}{\rho\:}\cdot\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} to the variance of the y-coordinate, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}}^{2}$$\end{document}, is on the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:-1{0}^{-4}$$\end{document}.

Compared with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}}^{2}$$\end{document} themselves, the effects introduced by the covariance terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} are relatively small. For the sake of analytical convenience, the influence of the covariance terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{x}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:{y}_{S}}^{2}$$\end{document} on the variances \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{x}_{P}}^{2}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{y}_{P}}^{2}$$\end{document} of the observation points is therefore neglected.

For the three measurement schemes proposed on the basis of the permissible attitude-control limits, Schemes I and II were evaluated using the design parameters listed in Table 3 and substituted into Eq. (29). The results show that, when station-point coordinate errors are neglected, the x-direction standard deviations of the four measurement and control points induced solely by instrument measurement errors exceed 2 mm and 1 mm, respectively, and thus fail to satisfy the limiting requirements derived earlier. For Scheme III, Eq. (29) was used to compute, under different orientation angle precisions, the weakest admissible station-point coordinate accuracy for each station point—that is, the maximum allowable standard deviation of the station-point coordinates that still satisfies the required measurement and control point coordinate accuracy (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}x}=1mm$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}y}=1mm$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{{P}_{n}z}=0.7mm$$\end{document}) for the corresponding orientation angle precision. The results are summarised in Table 6.

Scheme III: station-point accuracy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{xS}\le\:0.7mm$$\end{document}、\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{yS}\le\:0.9mm$$\end{document}、\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{zS}\le\:0.6mm$$\end{document}、\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}\le\:1{\prime\:\prime\:}$$\end{document}.

Scheme III: station-point accuracy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{xS}\le\:0.7mm$$\end{document}、\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{yS}\le\:0.9mm$$\end{document}、\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{zS}\le\:0.6mm$$\end{document}、\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}\le\:1{\prime\:\prime\:}$$\end{document}.

By synthesising the results derived from the permissible measurement and control limits based on attitude parameters with those based on absolute position parameters, it can be concluded that, to meet the requirements of vacuum maglev tube support installation measurements, a TS with an angular measurement accuracy of 0.5″ and a distance measurement accuracy of 0.6 mm + 1 ppm should be employed. Under these conditions, a single station setup can be used to carry out installation measurements for tube supports on up to four bridge piers.

Offline control network configuration

The layout scheme of the offline control network is determined using a virtual simulation approach. In combination with actual site conditions and the configuration of the higher-order control network, the scheme can be flexibly selected on the basis of a comprehensive assessment of network strength. Simulation computation is then used to evaluate the achievable spatial distribution of station-point accuracy under different network layouts and precision conditions.

In this study, the current layout form of the HSR CPⅢ control network is taken as the basis, and an offline CFⅢ control network is constructed by enhancing its accuracy standards. Specifically, the layout of the offline CFⅢ control points is identical to that of the CPⅢ network. The same coordinate configuration as that used in the preceding simulation experiment design is adopted for the layout example, while the design accuracy is increased to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{x}={{\upsigma\:}}_{y}=1.2\mathrm{m}\mathrm{m}$$\end{document},\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{{\upsigma\:}}_{\mathrm{z}}=1.5\mathrm{m}\mathrm{m}$$\end{document}. Each station point is established with reference to four pairs of CFⅢ points located ahead of and behind the station. On this basis, virtual simulation computation is employed to analyse the station-point accuracy within the region and to evaluate the three station-point accuracy limit schemes proposed in the previous section. The simulation results are shown in Figs. 20, 21 and 22.

Fig. 20Simulation results for station-point accuracy limit Scheme I.

Simulation results for station-point accuracy limit Scheme I.

Fig. 21Simulation results for station-point accuracy limit Scheme II.

Simulation results for station-point accuracy limit Scheme II.

Fig. 22Simulation results for station-point accuracy limit Scheme III.

Simulation results for station-point accuracy limit Scheme III.

Based on the simulation results, when the above control network layout scheme is adopted, the differences among the three station-point accuracy limit schemes are manifested mainly in the standard deviation of the y-coordinate and the standard deviation of the orientation angle. In Scheme III, the excessively stringent limit imposed on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}$$\end{document} results in none of the station points within the region satisfying the required accuracy criteria. In contrast, Scheme II, relative to Scheme I, achieves a moderate relaxation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{y}$$\end{document} by tightening the limit on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{\sigma\:}_{\omega\:}$$\end{document}, thereby expanding the area in which station points meet the accuracy requirements. Consequently, under the given offline control network configuration, Scheme II is recommended as the limiting standard for station-point accuracy.

Based on the above analyses, a comprehensive accuracy framework is established through rigorous derivation and computation. Starting from the absolute position and attitude design parameters of ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev tube supports, the permissible accuracy limits for installation measurements at the fixture measurement and control points are derived. These are further propagated to obtain the accuracy limits for 3D free-station positioning with TS that explicitly account for the precision of known points. Finally, simulation computations are employed to validate the rationality of the offline control network layout and its associated accuracy design. Together, these elements constitute an integrated accuracy control system for the installation measurement of ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev tube supports, with explicit consideration of known-point precision.

Based on the above calculations, the installation measurement accuracy control system for the tube supports in the case study considered in this work can be summarised as follows:

The offline CFⅢ control network adopts a point-pair configuration, with a spacing of 60 m between adjacent point pairs and a transverse separation of 20 m between the two control points within each pair. In summary, the “reverse” accuracy allocation method proposed in this study establishes a rigorous closed loop of accuracy transfer in this case study. Starting from the design accuracy requirements of the maglev supports, the method progressively back-calculates the required accuracies of the measurement and control points and the free-station setup, and ultimately anchors these requirements to the configuration and accuracy indices of the offline control network. This hierarchical quantitative derivation mechanism not only defines a complete technical pathway for satisfying engineering requirements, but also establishes quantitative thresholds for control network configuration, station-region planning and on-site measurement and control, thereby replacing the conventional design approach that relies largely on empirical judgement. The proposed method provides a high-confidence theoretical basis for measurement and control scheme design, ensuring the scientific validity and precision of field construction parameters and effectively mitigating construction risks arising from mismatched accuracy allocation.

Conclusions

The polymorphic coupled rail transit dynamic model test platform, characterised by its ultra-high design speed and extremely complex experimental environment, imposes exceptionally stringent requirements on measurement operations. As the fundamental load-bearing elements of the vacuum tube system, the installation accuracy of tube supports directly governs the geometric precision and spatial attitude of the entire tube system. However, this field currently lacks mature technical standards and methodological frameworks, posing substantial technical challenges for support installation measurements. To address the absence of systematic theoretical derivations in installation measurement and control and the resulting difficulty in formulating rational measurement schemes, this study was conducted. The main conclusions are summarised as follows.This study proposes a support installation measurement and control accuracy derivation method based on a “reverse” accuracy allocation strategy. Starting from the design accuracy requirements of the supports, the method integrates the law of error propagation, the principle of LSC adjustment, and computer-based virtual simulation techniques to establish a complete accuracy transfer chain—from support design parameters to the accuracy of prism measurement and control points on the installation fixture, to station-point accuracy, and ultimately to the accuracy of the offline control network. The proposed approach exhibits strong generality and is applicable to a wide range of support parameters, instrument observation precisions and control network layout schemes.

This study demonstrates, through theoretical derivation, that the absolute position accuracy of support installation is jointly influenced by station-point accuracy and observation accuracy, whereas the attitude control accuracy is governed primarily by measurement accuracy and the orientation angle accuracy of the station point. These findings provide a rigorous theoretical basis for error control in engineering practice.

This study establishes a 3D free-station adjustment model for TS based on the LSC principle that explicitly incorporates the prior precision of known points. In this model, control points are treated as signals with associated accuracy information in the adjustment process, effectively suppressing the influence of known-point errors on station-point estimation. Simulation experiments demonstrate that, under extreme error scenarios, the proposed model consistently achieves higher adjustment accuracy than conventional Ordinary Least Squares methods.

This study reveals the spatial distribution characteristics of station-point accuracy through dense grid-based simulation experiments, showing that station-point accuracy is governed primarily by the planar configuration of control points and exhibits no significant dependence on station elevation. This finding provides practical guidance for the selection of station locations in the field, indicating that, to maintain a favourable field of view for installation measurements, station regions should preferentially be located on the tops of bridge piers.

Through a case study, the feasibility and practical applicability of the proposed accuracy derivation method are verified, providing both theoretical support and a representative reference for the design of installation measurement schemes for ultra-high-speed vacuum maglev tube supports.
